I work around boxes and inventory all day, so I’m used to counting things, keeping track, making sure stuff goes where it’s supposed to… but with food at night, I don’t seem to have that same control. That’s what’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started, even if you want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s the part that bothers me. I’ll start just picking at something late at night, and I’m saying to myself, okay, that’s enough, Santiago, you’re done. But then my hand goes back again.\n\nIt’s like when I’m at work and I know exactly where a box is supposed to go, you know, there’s a system, there’s a label… but with this, it doesn’t feel like I’m the one deciding anymore. I want to stop, but I don’t always stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if guilty is the word I’d use. I mean… maybe sometimes I feel annoyed with myself, like, “Why did you do that?” But guilt sounds heavier, you know?\n\nIt’s more like when I mess up an order at work and I keep thinking about how I should’ve caught it before it went out. Then I start going over it in my head, and sometimes I do that with the eating too, but… I’m not sure if that’s guilt exactly.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant change in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, my pants feel… maybe the same? Maybe a little tighter some days, but that could just be laundry shrinking them, you know? My wife used to say I always blame the dryer.\n\nI don’t weigh myself much. Yeah… I mean, not every time, but some nights it’s a lot more than I want to admit.

Like after work, if there’s tamales left, I can go through several of them, and then there’s pozole in the pot, so I’ll have a big bowl of that too. Then I start going over it in my head, and sometimes I do that with the eating too, but… I’m not sure if that’s guilt exactly.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant change in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, my pants feel… maybe the same? Maybe a little tighter some days, but that could just be laundry shrinking them, you know? My wife used to say I always blame the dryer.\n\nI don’t weigh myself much. At work we’ve got scales for boxes and shipments, and I can tell you exactly if something’s off by a pound there, but my own weight… I don’t really keep track like that. So I can’t say I’ve noticed a big change, no.\n\nDoctor: How often do these late-night eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: It’s been… I guess most weeks, at least once. Not every single night or anything like that.\n\nUsually it’s one of those nights where there’s leftovers in the fridge, and everybody’s already gone to bed. That’s kind of when it happens. Like if there’s a pan of something from dinner, I’ll tell myself I’m just putting it away better, or just taking a little bit, and then… yeah. For the past several months, it’s been like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating very quickly during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I do eat pretty fast when it happens. Not like dinner with everyone, where you’re talking and passing stuff around.\n\nIt’s more like I’m standing there in the kitchen and I’m trying to get it over with before anyone comes down or wakes up. Like, I’ll barely even taste it sometimes. Which is kind of stupid, because the whole point is eating it, right?\n\nAt work, if I’m rushing a shipment, I get that same feeling, like just finish it quick before somebody notices it’s late. It’s sort of like that, I guess. Fast, yeah.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of what you're eating during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s the part that bothers me, honestly.\n\nI’ll start just picking at something, like just a bite or two, and I’ll tell myself, “That’s enough, Santiago,” but then my hand just keeps going back. It feels like I already decided to stop, but somehow I’m still doing it anyway.\n\nIt’s not like when I’m counting inventory, where you can just say, okay, stop at this number and that’s it. With the food, late at night, it’s like I don’t really get to make the decision anymore, at least for a little while.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat a large amount of food during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not every time, but some nights it’s a lot more than I want to admit.\n\nLike after work, if there’s tamales left, I can go through several of them, and then there’s pozole in the pot, so I’ll have a big bowl of that too. And then somehow I’m grabbing tortillas, and if there’s rice or beans still sitting there, I’ll tell myself I’m just clearing out the containers or making room in the fridge.\n\nI’ll be standing there in the kitchen like I’m “cleaning up,” but really I’m eating a whole extra meal… maybe more. Then later I look at what’s gone and think, man, that was a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating more than you physically wanted to during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I think so. By the time I finally stop, it’s like my stomach is tight, heavy, like I pushed it past where I meant to.\n\nSometimes I’ll just sit on the edge of the couch for a while after, because if I bend over or try to lie down, it feels bad. And then I’m sitting there thinking about stupid stuff, like how I still need to fold the laundry or whether I left the garage light on, but really I’m just waiting for my stomach to settle. So… yeah, more than I physically wanted to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I don’t know if I’d put it that way exactly. I mean, everybody’s stressed, right? Work gets hectic sometimes, shipments come in wrong, somebody miscounts a pallet, and then you’re chasing paperwork around half the day.

Yeah… that’s the part that bothers me, honestly.

Because I’ll be standing there and I’ll even say to myself, like, “That’s enough, Santiago, just put it away.” And then I’m putting foil back on something, or moving the container, and somehow my hand goes right back in. Like I already decided to stop, but my body didn’t get the message.

It’s weird because at work I can keep track of a hundred little things—labels, counts, deliveries, all that—but then in my own kitchen at night, with leftovers, it feels like I don’t really get to make the decision anymore.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.4, F50.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess it’s the eating. Mostly late at night. I’ll start picking at something, you know, standing there in the kitchen, and I tell myself, “That’s enough, Santiago,” but then my hand just keeps going back. Like I’m not really the one making the decision anymore.\n\nIt sounds kind of silly when I say it out loud. I work around boxes and inventory all day, so I’m used to counting things, keeping track, making sure stuff goes where it’s supposed to… but with food at night, I don’t seem to have that same control. That’s what’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started, even if you want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s the part that bothers me. I’ll start just picking at something late at night, and I’m saying to myself, okay, that’s enough, Santiago, you’re done. But then my hand goes back again.\n\nIt’s like when I’m at work and I know exactly where a box is supposed to go, you know, there’s a system, there’s a label… but with this, it doesn’t feel like I’m the one deciding anymore. I want to stop, but I don’t always stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if guilty is the word I’d use. I mean… maybe sometimes I feel annoyed with myself, like, “Why did you do that?” But guilt sounds heavier, you know?\n\nIt’s more like when I mess up an order at work and I keep thinking about how I should’ve caught it before it went out. Then I start going over it in my head, and sometimes I do that with the eating too, but… I’m not sure if that’s guilt exactly.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant change in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, my pants feel… maybe the same? Maybe a little tighter some days, but that could just be laundry shrinking them, you know? My wife used to say I always blame the dryer.\n\nI don’t weigh myself much. At work we’ve got scales for boxes and shipments, and I can tell you exactly if something’s off by a pound there, but my own weight… I don’t really keep track like that. So I can’t say I’ve noticed a big change, no.\n\nDoctor: How often do these late-night eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: It’s been… I guess most weeks, at least once. Fast, yeah.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of what you're eating during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s the part that bothers me, honestly.\n\nI’ll start just picking at something, like just a bite or two, and I’ll tell myself, “That’s enough, Santiago,” but then my hand just keeps going back. Like I already decided to stop, but my body didn’t get the message.\n\nIt’s weird because at work I can keep track of a hundred little things—labels, counts, deliveries, all that—but then in my own kitchen at night, with leftovers, it feels like I don’t really get to make the decision anymore.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I mean… guilty, ashamed… I don’t know. Maybe embarrassed that I did it again, if that counts. But I don’t really sit there crying or anything.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess it’s the eating. Mostly late at night. I’ll start picking at something, you know, standing there in the kitchen, and I tell myself, “That’s enough, Santiago,” but then my hand just keeps going back. Like I’m not really the one making the decision anymore.\n\nIt sounds kind of silly when I say it out loud. I work around boxes and inventory all day, so I’m used to counting things, keeping track, making sure stuff goes where it’s supposed to… but with food at night, I don’t seem to have that same control. That’s what’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started, even if you want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s the part that bothers me. I’ll start just picking at something late at night, and I’m saying to myself, okay, that’s enough, Santiago, you’re done. But then my hand goes back again.\n\nIt’s like when I’m at work and I know exactly where a box is supposed to go, you know, there’s a system, there’s a label… but with this, it doesn’t feel like I’m the one deciding anymore. I want to stop, but I don’t always stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if guilty is the word I’d use. Then I start going over it in my head, and sometimes I do that with the eating too, but… I’m not sure if that’s guilt exactly.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant change in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, my pants feel… maybe the same? Maybe a little tighter some days, but that could just be laundry shrinking them, you know? My wife used to say I always blame the dryer.\n\nI don’t weigh myself much. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess it’s the eating. Mostly late at night. I’ll start picking at something, you know, standing there in the kitchen, and I tell myself, “That’s enough, Santiago,” but then my hand just keeps going back. Like I’m not really the one making the decision anymore.\n\nIt sounds kind of silly when I say it out loud. I work around boxes and inventory all day, so I’m used to counting things, keeping track, making sure stuff goes where it’s supposed to… but with food at night, I don’t seem to have that same control. That’s what’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started, even if you want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s the part that bothers me. I’ll start just picking at something late at night, and I’m saying to myself, okay, that’s enough, Santiago, you’re done. But then my hand goes back again.\n\nIt’s like when I’m at work and I know exactly where a box is supposed to go, you know, there’s a system, there’s a label… but with this, it doesn’t feel like I’m the one deciding anymore. I want to stop, but I don’t always stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if guilty is the word I’d use. I mean… maybe sometimes I feel annoyed with myself, like, “Why did you do that?” But guilt sounds heavier, you know?\n\nIt’s more like when I mess up an order at work and I keep thinking about how I should’ve caught it before it went out. Then I start going over it in my head, and sometimes I do that with the eating too, but… I’m not sure if that’s guilt exactly.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant change in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, my pants feel… maybe the same? Maybe a little tighter some days, but that could just be laundry shrinking them, you know? My wife used to say I always blame the dryer.\n\nI don’t weigh myself much. At work we’ve got scales for boxes and shipments, and I can tell you exactly if something’s off by a pound there, but my own weight… I don’t really keep track like that. So I can’t say I’ve noticed a big change, no.\n\nDoctor: How often do these late-night eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: It’s been… I guess most weeks, at least once. Not every single night or anything like that.\n\nUsually it’s one of those nights where there’s leftovers in the fridge, and everybody’s already gone to bed. That’s kind of when it happens. Like if there’s a pan of something from dinner, I’ll tell myself I’m just putting it away better, or just taking a little bit, and then… yeah. For the past several months, it’s been like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating very quickly during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I do eat pretty fast when it happens. Fast, yeah.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of what you're eating during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s the part that bothers me, honestly.\n\nI’ll start just picking at something, like just a bite or two, and I’ll tell myself, “That’s enough, Santiago,” but then my hand just keeps going back. It feels like I already decided to stop, but somehow I’m still doing it anyway.\n\nIt’s not like when I’m counting inventory, where you can just say, okay, stop at this number and that’s it. Then later I look at what’s gone and think, man, that was a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating more than you physically wanted to during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I think so. By the time I finally stop, it’s like my stomach is tight, heavy, like I pushed it past where I meant to.\n\nSometimes I’ll just sit on the edge of the couch for a while after, because if I bend over or try to lie down, it feels bad. And then I’m sitting there thinking about stupid stuff, like how I still need to fold the laundry or whether I left the garage light on, but really I’m just waiting for my stomach to settle. With the food, late at night, it’s like I don’t really get to make the decision anymore, at least for a little while.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat a large amount of food during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not every time, but some nights it’s a lot more than I want to admit.\n\nLike after work, if there’s tamales left, I can go through several of them, and then there’s pozole in the pot, so I’ll have a big bowl of that too. And then somehow I’m grabbing tortillas, and if there’s rice or beans still sitting there, I’ll tell myself I’m just clearing out the containers or making room in the fridge.\n\nI’ll be standing there in the kitchen like I’m “cleaning up,” but really I’m eating a whole extra meal… maybe more. Then later I look at what’s gone and think, man, that was a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating more than you physically wanted to during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I think so. By the time I finally stop, it’s like my stomach is tight, heavy, like I pushed it past where I meant to.\n\nSometimes I’ll just sit on the edge of the couch for a while after, because if I bend over or try to lie down, it feels bad. And then I’m sitting there thinking about stupid stuff, like how I still need to fold the laundry or whether I left the garage light on, but really I’m just waiting for my stomach to settle. So… yeah, more than I physically wanted to.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat during these late-night episodes because you're feeling stressed, anxious, or upset?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it that way exactly. I mean, everybody’s stressed, right? Work gets hectic sometimes, shipments come in wrong, somebody miscounts a pallet, and then you’re chasing paperwork around half the day.\n\nBut with the late-night eating… I haven’t really noticed, like, “Oh, I’m anxious, so now I’m eating.” It’s more like I’m just there in the kitchen and it starts. Maybe I’m tired, or just not paying attention. I’m not sure if upset is the reason.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop yourself from continuing to eat during these episodes, even if you want to stop?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s the part that bothers me, honestly.\n\nBecause I’ll be standing there and I’ll even say to myself, like, “That’s enough, Santiago, just put it away.” And then I’m putting foil back on something, or moving the container, and somehow my hand goes right back in. Like I already decided to stop, but my body didn’t get the message.\n\nIt’s weird because at work I can keep track of a hundred little things—labels, counts, deliveries, all that—but then in my own kitchen at night, with leftovers, it feels like I don’t really get to make the decision anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or ashamed after these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I mean… guilty, ashamed… I don’t know. Maybe embarrassed that I did it again, if that counts. But I don’t really sit there crying or anything.\n\nIt’s more like I start thinking, “Well, that was stupid,” and then I get distracted by cleaning up the counter or checking if the trash needs to go out. Our trash pickup is early on Tuesdays, and if I forget, then the bin sits there full and my wife gets annoyed—well, not even annoyed exactly, just gives me that look.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I’d call it shame. I don’t feel great about it, but I’m not sure what word fits.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating during these episodes because you can't control yourself, or is it more like you're choosing to continue eating?\n\nPatient: It feels more like I can’t control it, yeah. I mean, I don’t like saying that, because I’m a grown man, you know? I make decisions all day—what goes where, what needs counting, who signed for what shipment.\n\nBut late at night, once I start picking at something, I’ll tell myself, “That’s enough, Santiago,” and I really mean it. Then I’m standing there with the container still open, and my hand just goes back in anyway. So I guess it doesn’t feel like I’m choosing exactly. It feels like the decision kind of gets away from me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I’ll start just picking at something late at night, and I’m saying to myself, okay, that’s enough, Santiago, you’re done. But then my hand goes back again.\n\nIt’s like when I’m at work and I know exactly where a box is supposed to go, you know, there’s a system, there’s a label… but with this, it doesn’t feel like I’m the one deciding anymore. I want to stop, but I don’t always stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if guilty is the word I’d use. I mean… maybe sometimes I feel annoyed with myself, like, “Why did you do that?” But guilt sounds heavier, you know?\n\nIt’s more like when I mess up an order at work and I keep thinking about how I should’ve caught it before it went out. Then I start going over it in my head, and sometimes I do that with the eating too, but… I’m not sure if that’s guilt exactly.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant change in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, my pants feel… maybe the same? Maybe a little tighter some days, but that could just be laundry shrinking them, you know? My wife used to say I always blame the dryer.\n\nI don’t weigh myself much. At work we’ve got scales for boxes and shipments, and I can tell you exactly if something’s off by a pound there, but my own weight… I don’t really keep track like that. So I can’t say I’ve noticed a big change, no.\n\nDoctor: How often do these late-night eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: It’s been… I guess most weeks, at least once. Not every single night or anything like that.\n\nUsually it’s one of those nights where there’s leftovers in the fridge, and everybody’s already gone to bed. That’s kind of when it happens. Like if there’s a pan of something from dinner, I’ll tell myself I’m just putting it away better, or just taking a little bit, and then… yeah. For the past several months, it’s been like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating very quickly during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I do eat pretty fast when it happens. Not like dinner with everyone, where you’re talking and passing stuff around.\n\nIt’s more like I’m standing there in the kitchen and I’m trying to get it over with before anyone comes down or wakes up. Like, I’ll barely even taste it sometimes. Which is kind of stupid, because the whole point is eating it, right?\n\nAt work, if I’m rushing a shipment, I get that same feeling, like just finish it quick before somebody notices it’s late. It’s sort of like that, I guess. Fast, yeah.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of what you're eating during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s the part that bothers me, honestly.\n\nI’ll start just picking at something, like just a bite or two, and I’ll tell myself, “That’s enough, Santiago,” but then my hand just keeps going back. It feels like I already decided to stop, but somehow I’m still doing it anyway.\n\nIt’s not like when I’m counting inventory, where you can just say, okay, stop at this number and that’s it. With the food, late at night, it’s like I don’t really get to make the decision anymore, at least for a little while.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat a large amount of food during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not every time, but some nights it’s a lot more than I want to admit.\n\nLike after work, if there’s tamales left, I can go through several of them, and then there’s pozole in the pot, so I’ll have a big bowl of that too. And then somehow I’m grabbing tortillas, and if there’s rice or beans still sitting there, I’ll tell myself I’m just clearing out the containers or making room in the fridge.\n\nI’ll be standing there in the kitchen like I’m “cleaning up,” but really I’m eating a whole extra meal… maybe more. Then later I look at what’s gone and think, man, that was a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating more than you physically wanted to during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I think so. By the time I finally stop, it’s like my stomach is tight, heavy, like I pushed it past where I meant to.\n\nSometimes I’ll just sit on the edge of the couch for a while after, because if I bend over or try to lie down, it feels bad. And then I’m sitting there thinking about stupid stuff, like how I still need to fold the laundry or whether I left the garage light on, but really I’m just waiting for my stomach to settle. So… yeah, more than I physically wanted to.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat during these late-night episodes because you're feeling stressed, anxious, or upset?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it that way exactly. I mean, everybody’s stressed, right? Work gets hectic sometimes, shipments come in wrong, somebody miscounts a pallet, and then you’re chasing paperwork around half the day.\n\nBut with the late-night eating… I haven’t really noticed, like, “Oh, I’m anxious, so now I’m eating.” It’s more like I’m just there in the kitchen and it starts. Maybe I’m tired, or just not paying attention. I’m not sure if upset is the reason.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop yourself from continuing to eat during these episodes, even if you want to stop?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s the part that bothers me, honestly.\n\nBecause I’ll be standing there and I’ll even say to myself, like, “That’s enough, Santiago, just put it away.” And then I’m putting foil back on something, or moving the container, and somehow my hand goes right back in. Like I already decided to stop, but my body didn’t get the message.\n\nIt’s weird because at work I can keep track of a hundred little things—labels, counts, deliveries, all that—but then in my own kitchen at night, with leftovers, it feels like I don’t really get to make the decision anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or ashamed after these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I mean… guilty, ashamed… I don’t know. Maybe embarrassed that I did it again, if that counts. But I don’t really sit there crying or anything.\n\nIt’s more like I start thinking, “Well, that was stupid,” and then I get distracted by cleaning up the counter or checking if the trash needs to go out. Our trash pickup is early on Tuesdays, and if I forget, then the bin sits there full and my wife gets annoyed—well, not even annoyed exactly, just gives me that look.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I’d call it shame. I don’t feel great about it, but I’m not sure what word fits.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating during these episodes because you can't control yourself, or is it more like you're choosing to continue eating?\n\nPatient: It feels more like I can’t control it, yeah. I mean, I don’t like saying that, because I’m a grown man, you know? I make decisions all day—what goes where, what needs counting, who signed for what shipment.\n\nBut late at night, once I start picking at something, I’ll tell myself, “That’s enough, Santiago,” and I really mean it. Then I’m standing there with the container still open, and my hand just goes back in anyway. So I guess it doesn’t feel like I’m choosing exactly. It feels like the decision kind of gets away from me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control what or how much you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s the thing. It’s not so much that I sit down and decide, like, “I’m going to eat all this.” It starts with just picking at something late at night, and then I’ll tell myself, “That’s enough, Santiago, close it up.”\n\nAnd then I don’t. My hand just keeps going back in, like I’m not really the one making the call anymore.\n\nI mean, it sounds silly when I say it out loud. At work I’m pretty good with keeping track of amounts and boxes and what’s supposed to go where, but with food at night… the amount kind of gets away from me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Like if there’s a pan of something from dinner, I’ll tell myself I’m just putting it away better, or just taking a little bit, and then… yeah. For the past several months, it’s been like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating very quickly during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I do eat pretty fast when it happens. Not like dinner with everyone, where you’re talking and passing stuff around.\n\nIt’s more like I’m standing there in the kitchen and I’m trying to get it over with before anyone comes down or wakes up. Like, I’ll barely even taste it sometimes. Which is kind of stupid, because the whole point is eating it, right?\n\nAt work, if I’m rushing a shipment, I get that same feeling, like just finish it quick before somebody notices it’s late. It’s sort of like that, I guess. Fast, yeah.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of what you're eating during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s the part that bothers me, honestly.\n\nI’ll start just picking at something, like just a bite or two, and I’ll tell myself, “That’s enough, Santiago,” but then my hand just keeps going back. It feels like I already decided to stop, but somehow I’m still doing it anyway.\n\nIt’s not like when I’m counting inventory, where you can just say, okay, stop at this number and that’s it. With the food, late at night, it’s like I don’t really get to make the decision anymore, at least for a little while.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat a large amount of food during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not every time, but some nights it’s a lot more than I want to admit.\n\nLike after work, if there’s tamales left, I can go through several of them, and then there’s pozole in the pot, so I’ll have a big bowl of that too. And then somehow I’m grabbing tortillas, and if there’s rice or beans still sitting there, I’ll tell myself I’m just clearing out the containers or making room in the fridge.\n\nI’ll be standing there in the kitchen like I’m “cleaning up,” but really I’m eating a whole extra meal… maybe more. Then later I look at what’s gone and think, man, that was a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating more than you physically wanted to during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I think so. By the time I finally stop, it’s like my stomach is tight, heavy, like I pushed it past where I meant to.\n\nSometimes I’ll just sit on the edge of the couch for a while after, because if I bend over or try to lie down, it feels bad. And then I’m sitting there thinking about stupid stuff, like how I still need to fold the laundry or whether I left the garage light on, but really I’m just waiting for my stomach to settle. So… yeah, more than I physically wanted to.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat during these late-night episodes because you're feeling stressed, anxious, or upset?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it that way exactly. I mean, everybody’s stressed, right? Work gets hectic sometimes, shipments come in wrong, somebody miscounts a pallet, and then you’re chasing paperwork around half the day.\n\nBut with the late-night eating… I haven’t really noticed, like, “Oh, I’m anxious, so now I’m eating.” It’s more like I’m just there in the kitchen and it starts. Maybe I’m tired, or just not paying attention. I’m not sure if upset is the reason.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop yourself from continuing to eat during these episodes, even if you want to stop?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s the part that bothers me, honestly.\n\nBecause I’ll be standing there and I’ll even say to myself, like, “That’s enough, Santiago, just put it away.” And then I’m putting foil back on something, or moving the container, and somehow my hand goes right back in. Like I already decided to stop, but my body didn’t get the message.\n\nIt’s weird because at work I can keep track of a hundred little things—labels, counts, deliveries, all that—but then in my own kitchen at night, with leftovers, it feels like I don’t really get to make the decision anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or ashamed after these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I mean… guilty, ashamed… I don’t know. Maybe embarrassed that I did it again, if that counts. But I don’t really sit there crying or anything.\n\nIt’s more like I start thinking, “Well, that was stupid,” and then I get distracted by cleaning up the counter or checking if the trash needs to go out. Our trash pickup is early on Tuesdays, and if I forget, then the bin sits there full and my wife gets annoyed—well, not even annoyed exactly, just gives me that look.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I’d call it shame. I don’t feel great about it, but I’m not sure what word fits.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating during these episodes because you can't control yourself, or is it more like you're choosing to continue eating?\n\nPatient: It feels more like I can’t control it, yeah. I mean, I don’t like saying that, because I’m a grown man, you know? I make decisions all day—what goes where, what needs counting, who signed for what shipment.\n\nBut late at night, once I start picking at something, I’ll tell myself, “That’s enough, Santiago,” and I really mean it. Then I’m standing there with the container still open, and my hand just goes back in anyway. So I guess it doesn’t feel like I’m choosing exactly. It feels like the decision kind of gets away from me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control what or how much you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s the thing. It’s not so much that I sit down and decide, like, “I’m going to eat all this.” It starts with just picking at something late at night, and then I’ll tell myself, “That’s enough, Santiago, close it up.”\n\nAnd then I don’t. My hand just keeps going back in, like I’m not really the one making the call anymore.\n\nI mean, it sounds silly when I say it out loud. At work I’m pretty good with keeping track of amounts and boxes and what’s supposed to go where, but with food at night… the amount kind of gets away from me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control your eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think that’s what worries me about it. It’s like once I start picking at something late at night, I’ll say to myself, “Alright, that’s enough, Santiago,” and I actually mean to stop.\n\nBut then my hand goes back again anyway, like I’m not really the one making the decision at that point. Which is strange, because at work I’m used to keeping count of things—boxes, inventory, whatever came in wrong on the truck—and I can be pretty careful. But with this, late at night, it kind of gets away from me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of how much you eat during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I’ll think I am at first, because it’s just picking at something late at night, like a little bit while I’m standing there.\n\nBut what worries me is I’ll tell myself, “That’s enough, Santiago,” and then my hand just keeps going back in anyway. It feels like I don’t get to make the decision anymore, if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s funny, not funny, but—at work I’m counting things all day, checking numbers, making sure shipments line up. So it bothers me that with food, I can’t seem to keep that same control.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. But then my hand goes back again.\n\nIt’s like when I’m at work and I know exactly where a box is supposed to go, you know, there’s a system, there’s a label… but with this, it doesn’t feel like I’m the one deciding anymore. Then I start going over it in my head, and sometimes I do that with the eating too, but… I’m not sure if that’s guilt exactly.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant change in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. So I can’t say I’ve noticed a big change, no.\n\nDoctor: How often do these late-night eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: It’s been… I guess most weeks, at least once. Not every single night or anything like that.\n\nUsually it’s one of those nights where there’s leftovers in the fridge, and everybody’s already gone to bed. That’s kind of when it happens. Like if there’s a pan of something from dinner, I’ll tell myself I’m just putting it away better, or just taking a little bit, and then… yeah. For the past several months, it’s been like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating very quickly during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I do eat pretty fast when it happens. Not like dinner with everyone, where you’re talking and passing stuff around.\n\nIt’s more like I’m standing there in the kitchen and I’m trying to get it over with before anyone comes down or wakes up. Like, I’ll barely even taste it sometimes. It’s sort of like that, I guess. Fast, yeah.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of what you're eating during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s the part that bothers me, honestly.\n\nI’ll start just picking at something, like just a bite or two, and I’ll tell myself, “That’s enough, Santiago,” but then my hand just keeps going back. It feels like I already decided to stop, but somehow I’m still doing it anyway.\n\nIt’s not like when I’m counting inventory, where you can just say, okay, stop at this number and that’s it. And then somehow I’m grabbing tortillas, and if there’s rice or beans still sitting there, I’ll tell myself I’m just clearing out the containers or making room in the fridge.\n\nI’ll be standing there in the kitchen like I’m “cleaning up,” but really I’m eating a whole extra meal… maybe more. Then later I look at what’s gone and think, man, that was a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating more than you physically wanted to during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I think so. By the time I finally stop, it’s like my stomach is tight, heavy, like I pushed it past where I meant to.\n\nSometimes I’ll just sit on the edge of the couch for a while after, because if I bend over or try to lie down, it feels bad. And then I’m sitting there thinking about stupid stuff, like how I still need to fold the laundry or whether I left the garage light on, but really I’m just waiting for my stomach to settle. So… yeah, more than I physically wanted to.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat during these late-night episodes because you're feeling stressed, anxious, or upset?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it that way exactly. I mean, everybody’s stressed, right? Work gets hectic sometimes, shipments come in wrong, somebody miscounts a pallet, and then you’re chasing paperwork around half the day.\n\nBut with the late-night eating… I haven’t really noticed, like, “Oh, I’m anxious, so now I’m eating.” It’s more like I’m just there in the kitchen and it starts. Maybe I’m tired, or just not paying attention. I’m not sure if upset is the reason.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop yourself from continuing to eat during these episodes, even if you want to stop?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s the part that bothers me, honestly.\n\nBecause I’ll be standing there and I’ll even say to myself, like, “That’s enough, Santiago, just put it away.” And then I’m putting foil back on something, or moving the container, and somehow my hand goes right back in. I work around boxes and inventory all day, so I’m used to counting things, keeping track, making sure stuff goes where it’s supposed to… but with food at night, I don’t seem to have that same control. That’s what’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started, even if you want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s the part that bothers me. I’ll start just picking at something late at night, and I’m saying to myself, okay, that’s enough, Santiago, you’re done. But then my hand goes back again.\n\nIt’s like when I’m at work and I know exactly where a box is supposed to go, you know, there’s a system, there’s a label… but with this, it doesn’t feel like I’m the one deciding anymore. I want to stop, but I don’t always stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if guilty is the word I’d use. I mean… maybe sometimes I feel annoyed with myself, like, “Why did you do that?” But guilt sounds heavier, you know?\n\nIt’s more like when I mess up an order at work and I keep thinking about how I should’ve caught it before it went out. Then I start going over it in my head, and sometimes I do that with the eating too, but… I’m not sure if that’s guilt exactly.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant change in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, my pants feel… maybe the same? Maybe a little tighter some days, but that could just be laundry shrinking them, you know? My wife used to say I always blame the dryer.\n\nI don’t weigh myself much. At work we’ve got scales for boxes and shipments, and I can tell you exactly if something’s off by a pound there, but my own weight… I don’t really keep track like that. So I can’t say I’ve noticed a big change, no.\n\nDoctor: How often do these late-night eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: It’s been… I guess most weeks, at least once. Not every single night or anything like that.\n\nUsually it’s one of those nights where there’s leftovers in the fridge, and everybody’s already gone to bed. That’s kind of when it happens. Like if there’s a pan of something from dinner, I’ll tell myself I’m just putting it away better, or just taking a little bit, and then… yeah. For the past several months, it’s been like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating very quickly during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I do eat pretty fast when it happens. Not like dinner with everyone, where you’re talking and passing stuff around.\n\nIt’s more like I’m standing there in the kitchen and I’m trying to get it over with before anyone comes down or wakes up. Like, I’ll barely even taste it sometimes. Which is kind of stupid, because the whole point is eating it, right?\n\nAt work, if I’m rushing a shipment, I get that same feeling, like just finish it quick before somebody notices it’s late. It’s sort of like that, I guess. Fast, yeah.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of what you're eating during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s the part that bothers me, honestly.\n\nI’ll start just picking at something, like just a bite or two, and I’ll tell myself, “That’s enough, Santiago,” but then my hand just keeps going back. It feels like I already decided to stop, but somehow I’m still doing it anyway.\n\nIt’s not like when I’m counting inventory, where you can just say, okay, stop at this number and that’s it. With the food, late at night, it’s like I don’t really get to make the decision anymore, at least for a little while.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat a large amount of food during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not every time, but some nights it’s a lot more than I want to admit.\n\nLike after work, if there’s tamales left, I can go through several of them, and then there’s pozole in the pot, so I’ll have a big bowl of that too. And then somehow I’m grabbing tortillas, and if there’s rice or beans still sitting there, I’ll tell myself I’m just clearing out the containers or making room in the fridge.\n\nI’ll be standing there in the kitchen like I’m “cleaning up,” but really I’m eating a whole extra meal… maybe more. Then later I look at what’s gone and think, man, that was a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating more than you physically wanted to during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I think so. By the time I finally stop, it’s like my stomach is tight, heavy, like I pushed it past where I meant to.\n\nSometimes I’ll just sit on the edge of the couch for a while after, because if I bend over or try to lie down, it feels bad. And then I’m sitting there thinking about stupid stuff, like how I still need to fold the laundry or whether I left the garage light on, but really I’m just waiting for my stomach to settle. So… yeah, more than I physically wanted to.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat during these late-night episodes because you're feeling stressed, anxious, or upset?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it that way exactly. I mean, everybody’s stressed, right? Work gets hectic sometimes, shipments come in wrong, somebody miscounts a pallet, and then you’re chasing paperwork around half the day.\n\nBut with the late-night eating… I haven’t really noticed, like, “Oh, I’m anxious, so now I’m eating.” It’s more like I’m just there in the kitchen and it starts. Maybe I’m tired, or just not paying attention. I’m not sure if upset is the reason.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop yourself from continuing to eat during these episodes, even if you want to stop?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s the part that bothers me, honestly.\n\nBecause I’ll be standing there and I’ll even say to myself, like, “That’s enough, Santiago, just put it away.” And then I’m putting foil back on something, or moving the container, and somehow my hand goes right back in. Like I already decided to stop, but my body didn’t get the message.\n\nIt’s weird because at work I can keep track of a hundred little things—labels, counts, deliveries, all that—but then in my own kitchen at night, with leftovers, it feels like I don’t really get to make the decision anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or ashamed after these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I mean… guilty, ashamed… I don’t know. Maybe embarrassed that I did it again, if that counts. But I don’t really sit there crying or anything.\n\nIt’s more like I start thinking, “Well, that was stupid,” and then I get distracted by cleaning up the counter or checking if the trash needs to go out. Our trash pickup is early on Tuesdays, and if I forget, then the bin sits there full and my wife gets annoyed—well, not even annoyed exactly, just gives me that look.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I’d call it shame. I don’t feel great about it, but I’m not sure what word fits.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating during these episodes because you can't control yourself, or is it more like you're choosing to continue eating?\n\nPatient: It feels more like I can’t control it, yeah. I mean, I don’t like saying that, because I’m a grown man, you know? I make decisions all day—what goes where, what needs counting, who signed for what shipment.\n\nBut late at night, once I start picking at something, I’ll tell myself, “That’s enough, Santiago,” and I really mean it. Then I’m standing there with the container still open, and my hand just goes back in anyway. So I guess it doesn’t feel like I’m choosing exactly. It feels like the decision kind of gets away from me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control what or how much you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s the thing. It’s not so much that I sit down and decide, like, “I’m going to eat all this.” It starts with just picking at something late at night, and then I’ll tell myself, “That’s enough, Santiago, close it up.”\n\nAnd then I don’t. My hand just keeps going back in, like I’m not really the one making the call anymore.\n\nI mean, it sounds silly when I say it out loud. At work I’m pretty good with keeping track of amounts and boxes and what’s supposed to go where, but with food at night… the amount kind of gets away from me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control your eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think that’s what worries me about it. Like I already decided to stop, but my body didn’t get the message.\n\nIt’s weird because at work I can keep track of a hundred little things—labels, counts, deliveries, all that—but then in my own kitchen at night, with leftovers, it feels like I don’t really get to make the decision anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or ashamed after these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I mean… guilty, ashamed… I don’t know. Maybe embarrassed that I did it again, if that counts. But I don’t really sit there crying or anything.\n\nIt’s more like I start thinking, “Well, that was stupid,” and then I get distracted by cleaning up the counter or checking if the trash needs to go out. Our trash pickup is early on Tuesdays, and if I forget, then the bin sits there full and my wife gets annoyed—well, not even annoyed exactly, just gives me that look.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I’d call it shame. I don’t feel great about it, but I’m not sure what word fits.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating during these episodes because you can't control yourself, or is it more like you're choosing to continue eating?\n\nPatient: It feels more like I can’t control it, yeah. I mean, I don’t like saying that, because I’m a grown man, you know? I make decisions all day—what goes where, what needs counting, who signed for what shipment.\n\nBut late at night, once I start picking at something, I’ll tell myself, “That’s enough, Santiago,” and I really mean it. Then I’m standing there with the container still open, and my hand just goes back in anyway. So I guess it doesn’t feel like I’m choosing exactly. It feels like the decision kind of gets away from me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control what or how much you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s the thing. It’s not so much that I sit down and decide, like, “I’m going to eat all this.” It starts with just picking at something late at night, and then I’ll tell myself, “That’s enough, Santiago, close it up.”\n\nAnd then I don’t. My hand just keeps going back in, like I’m not really the one making the call anymore.\n\nI mean, it sounds silly when I say it out loud. At work I’m pretty good with keeping track of amounts and boxes and what’s supposed to go where, but with food at night… the amount kind of gets away from me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control your eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think that’s what worries me about it. Which is strange, because at work I’m used to keeping count of things—boxes, inventory, whatever came in wrong on the truck—and I can be pretty careful. It feels like I don’t get to make the decision anymore, if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s funny, not funny, but—at work I’m counting things all day, checking numbers, making sure shipments line up. So it bothers me that with food, I can’t seem to keep that same control.\n\nDoctor: How many nights a week would you say you have one of these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Most weeks it’s at least one night, I’d say. So I can’t say I’ve noticed a big change, no.\n\nDoctor: How often do these late-night eating episodes happen?\n\nPatient: It’s been… I guess most weeks, at least once. Not every single night or anything like that.\n\nUsually it’s one of those nights where there’s leftovers in the fridge, and everybody’s already gone to bed. That’s kind of when it happens. Like if there’s a pan of something from dinner, I’ll tell myself I’m just putting it away better, or just taking a little bit, and then… yeah. For the past several months, it’s been like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating very quickly during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I do eat pretty fast when it happens. Not like dinner with everyone, where you’re talking and passing stuff around.\n\nIt’s more like I’m standing there in the kitchen and I’m trying to get it over with before anyone comes down or wakes up. Like, I’ll barely even taste it sometimes. Which is kind of stupid, because the whole point is eating it, right?\n\nAt work, if I’m rushing a shipment, I get that same feeling, like just finish it quick before somebody notices it’s late. It’s sort of like that, I guess. Fast, yeah.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of what you're eating during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s the part that bothers me, honestly.\n\nI’ll start just picking at something, like just a bite or two, and I’ll tell myself, “That’s enough, Santiago,” but then my hand just keeps going back. It feels like I already decided to stop, but somehow I’m still doing it anyway.\n\nIt’s not like when I’m counting inventory, where you can just say, okay, stop at this number and that’s it. With the food, late at night, it’s like I don’t really get to make the decision anymore, at least for a little while.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat a large amount of food during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not every time, but some nights it’s a lot more than I want to admit.\n\nLike after work, if there’s tamales left, I can go through several of them, and then there’s pozole in the pot, so I’ll have a big bowl of that too. And then somehow I’m grabbing tortillas, and if there’s rice or beans still sitting there, I’ll tell myself I’m just clearing out the containers or making room in the fridge.\n\nI’ll be standing there in the kitchen like I’m “cleaning up,” but really I’m eating a whole extra meal… maybe more. Then later I look at what’s gone and think, man, that was a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating more than you physically wanted to during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I think so. By the time I finally stop, it’s like my stomach is tight, heavy, like I pushed it past where I meant to.\n\nSometimes I’ll just sit on the edge of the couch for a while after, because if I bend over or try to lie down, it feels bad. And then I’m sitting there thinking about stupid stuff, like how I still need to fold the laundry or whether I left the garage light on, but really I’m just waiting for my stomach to settle. So… yeah, more than I physically wanted to.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat during these late-night episodes because you're feeling stressed, anxious, or upset?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it that way exactly. I mean, everybody’s stressed, right? Work gets hectic sometimes, shipments come in wrong, somebody miscounts a pallet, and then you’re chasing paperwork around half the day.\n\nBut with the late-night eating… I haven’t really noticed, like, “Oh, I’m anxious, so now I’m eating.” It’s more like I’m just there in the kitchen and it starts. Maybe I’m tired, or just not paying attention. I’m not sure if upset is the reason.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop yourself from continuing to eat during these episodes, even if you want to stop?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s the part that bothers me, honestly.\n\nBecause I’ll be standing there and I’ll even say to myself, like, “That’s enough, Santiago, just put it away.” And then I’m putting foil back on something, or moving the container, and somehow my hand goes right back in. Like I already decided to stop, but my body didn’t get the message.\n\nIt’s weird because at work I can keep track of a hundred little things—labels, counts, deliveries, all that—but then in my own kitchen at night, with leftovers, it feels like I don’t really get to make the decision anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or ashamed after these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I mean… guilty, ashamed… I don’t know. Maybe embarrassed that I did it again, if that counts. But I don’t really sit there crying or anything.\n\nIt’s more like I start thinking, “Well, that was stupid,” and then I get distracted by cleaning up the counter or checking if the trash needs to go out. Our trash pickup is early on Tuesdays, and if I forget, then the bin sits there full and my wife gets annoyed—well, not even annoyed exactly, just gives me that look.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I’d call it shame. I don’t feel great about it, but I’m not sure what word fits.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating during these episodes because you can't control yourself, or is it more like you're choosing to continue eating?\n\nPatient: It feels more like I can’t control it, yeah. I mean, I don’t like saying that, because I’m a grown man, you know? I make decisions all day—what goes where, what needs counting, who signed for what shipment.\n\nBut late at night, once I start picking at something, I’ll tell myself, “That’s enough, Santiago,” and I really mean it. Then I’m standing there with the container still open, and my hand just goes back in anyway. So I guess it doesn’t feel like I’m choosing exactly. It feels like the decision kind of gets away from me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control what or how much you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s the thing. It’s not so much that I sit down and decide, like, “I’m going to eat all this.” It starts with just picking at something late at night, and then I’ll tell myself, “That’s enough, Santiago, close it up.”\n\nAnd then I don’t. My hand just keeps going back in, like I’m not really the one making the call anymore.\n\nI mean, it sounds silly when I say it out loud. At work I’m pretty good with keeping track of amounts and boxes and what’s supposed to go where, but with food at night… the amount kind of gets away from me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control your eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think that’s what worries me about it. It’s like once I start picking at something late at night, I’ll say to myself, “Alright, that’s enough, Santiago,” and I actually mean to stop.\n\nBut then my hand goes back again anyway, like I’m not really the one making the decision at that point. Which is strange, because at work I’m used to keeping count of things—boxes, inventory, whatever came in wrong on the truck—and I can be pretty careful. But with this, late at night, it kind of gets away from me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of how much you eat during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I’ll think I am at first, because it’s just picking at something late at night, like a little bit while I’m standing there.\n\nBut what worries me is I’ll tell myself, “That’s enough, Santiago,” and then my hand just keeps going back in anyway. It feels like I don’t get to make the decision anymore, if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s funny, not funny, but—at work I’m counting things all day, checking numbers, making sure shipments line up. So it bothers me that with food, I can’t seem to keep that same control.\n\nDoctor: How many nights a week would you say you have one of these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Most weeks it’s at least one night, I’d say. Not every single night or anything, but for the past several months there’s usually been one night where it happens.\n\nIt’s more likely if there are leftovers around, you know, like if we had a big dinner and everyone else has gone to bed. I’ll be in the kitchen and it’s quiet, and then… yeah, that’s when it tends to happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop yourself from eating once you've started during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s pretty much it. I’ll start with just a bite or picking at something, and then I’ll tell myself, “Okay, that’s enough, Santiago,” like I’m done.\n\nBut then my hand goes back anyway. It’s like I’m standing there watching myself do it, almost. I don’t know, it reminds me of when I’m at work and you keep counting the same pallet because the numbers don’t feel right, except this is different because I’m not fixing anything. I’m just… eating more even though I already decided to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control yourself when eating during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I know I kind of said it already, but that’s the part that scares me. Once I start picking at something late at night, I’ll say to myself, “That’s enough, Santiago,” and then… my hand just keeps going back.\n\nIt’s like the decision’s already been made somewhere else, not by me. Which sounds dramatic, I know, but that’s how it feels in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have been able to stop eating if you weren't feeling any emotional distress during those late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe, but I don’t really know. It’s hard to separate it out like that, because late at night everything kind of feels heavier anyway, you know? The house is quiet, and I start thinking about stuff, or I’m just tired.\n\nBut the thing that worries me is once I start picking at the food, I’ll tell myself, “That’s enough, Santiago,” and then my hand keeps going back. Like I don’t get to make the decision anymore. Even if I wasn’t upset, I’m not sure I’d just stop easy once it starts.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
